## KSI-IAM-APM — Implemented

### Implementation

- The statement is conditional with a provider-defined escape hatch, so satisfying it means stating a feasibility position an assessor can test, not asserting passwordless. Human identity is federated: Google Cloud Identity is the workforce identity provider and AWS IAM Identity Center accepts its SAML assertion, so authentication policy is configured and enforced in one place rather than twice. The position here: passwordless is feasible for every federated human identity, because Cloud Identity supports passkeys and security keys natively and there are no legacy clients, shared workstations, or unmanaged devices. AWS root is the sole genuine infeasibility, since root sign-in is a username and password flow by platform design, and it deliberately remains native so that console access survives an outage of the identity provider; it sits in the fallback lane and must satisfy both halves of it. Strong is undefined, so the fallback binds to the NIST SP 800-63B position the mapped IA-5 controls point at, length-driven rather than composition-driven, screened against known-breached secrets, no forced periodic rotation; citing the source makes the choice testable rather than arbitrary. Phishing-resistant is undefined and governs only the fallback lane: where passwordless is in force, origin binding is inherent to passkeys and security keys, so the interpretive question is confined to what counts as a fallback factor, and the answer is a hardware or platform authenticator rather than TOTP or SMS, which are defeatable by real-time relay. Because the factor is presented to the identity provider, AWS holds no enrollment record for federated users, so AWS-side credential evidence is scoped to root and any break-glass IAM user. Authentication and authorization is bounded by the neighbouring indicators: account lifecycle belongs to AAM, privilege scope to ELP and JIT, and non-user identities to SNU for their credentials and to SUS where they hold privileged capability, leaving APM the credential and factor a human uses to authenticate and elevate. Fails if a passwordless method is available and unused with no stated reason, if a fallback identity lacks phishing-resistant MFA or a conforming password policy, if any accepted method is neither passwordless nor phishing-resistant MFA, or if the stated feasibility position does not match observed configuration. One identity failing fails the indicator, with no percentage threshold.
- **1. IAM Identity Center authentication** — Passwordless via WebAuthn or passkey for all human identities. MFA required every sign-in, not context-aware. Virtual TOTP not permitted. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. AWS root credential** — Fallback lane. Hardware security key registered. Password meeting the cited SP 800-63B position. *(AWS; provisioned by Manual, declared)*
- **3. Cloud Identity authentication** — Passwordless or security-key-only 2SV enforced at organization scope, no exempted OU. TOTP and SMS disallowed. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*

Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Authentication method configuration and permitted factor types. Source: AWS IAM Identity Center API. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-APM.verify.1`: not automated -- gap. Sign-in is delegated to Google Cloud Identity through SAML, so the factor is Google's 2-step verification, configured in Google Admin. Identity Center's own MFA settings do not apply to an external identity provider, and nothing reads Google's. Risk: The factor policy rests on the Google Admin setting, unverified by machine; a change to it there would not be seen here.*
- **CFG** — 2SV enforcement setting and OU scope, confirming no exempted population. Source: GCP Admin SDK. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-APM.verify.2`: not automated -- gap. Google's 2SV enforcement is in the Admin console, reached by the Admin SDK with directory admin consent the collector does not hold. The operator's account signs in with a passkey (DECISIONS.md, 2026-09-29); enforcement across the OU is not verified. Risk: An account in the OU without 2SV would not be noticed by any check.*
- **CFG** — Registered credential type per identity, root enumerated separately. Source: AWS iam list-mfa-devices and list-virtual-mfa-devices; GCP Directory API. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-APM.verify.3`: partly automated by `iam-apm-cfg-aws-root-credentials`. Not covered: Root alone. There are no IAM users (iam-snu-cfg-aws-no-user-access-keys), and the operator's factor is Google's 2-step verification behind SAML, which AWS cannot enumerate; GCP's identities' factors are not read.*

Google sign-in events are in the Admin audit log, which nothing collects; GCP's Cloud Audit Logs are not in the corpus either (registers/event-sources.yaml). Risk: The factor used at a GCP sign-in is not observed.*
- **OPS** — Every human identity in both clouds appears in the credential inventory, zero unaccounted. Source: Reconciliation against the inventory generated for KSI-PIY-GIV. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-APM.validate.3`: partly automated by `iam-elp-ops-identities-placed`. Not covered: Non-human identities only. The human identities -- the Identity Center user, the Google account, root -- are not inventoried types; the register lists them, and the APM checks judge their credentials.*

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by registering a known non-compliant factor on a disposable identity and confirming the collector fails it, then removing it and confirming the collector passes. Sufficiency is bounded by what each provider's API exposes about factor type: where an API reports that MFA is present without naming the method, that identity is reported as indeterminate rather than passed. Not knowing is reported as not knowing.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Root is configured manually and asserted, not declared in code, so its state is verified but not reproducible from the repository. Two independent identity stores with no central provider means an identity present in one cloud and absent from the other, or offboarded from one only, is a gap this architecture carries; that is a persona decision rather than a framework gap and belongs to AAM. The feasibility position is this project's own and is the part most open to challenge: an assessor could reasonably argue passwordless was feasible for an identity placed in the fallback lane.

- Negative control for `iam-apm-ops-aws-console-sign-ins-use-mfa` (collector/self_test.py): inputs that must fail it: no rows, query failed, query failed, zero expected, rows where none expected, unknown expectation.

## KSI-IAM-SNU — Implemented

### Implementation

- Appropriately secure is undefined and the mapped controls do not point anywhere specific enough to borrow from, so the standard is constructed here as a hierarchy. Best is no credential at all, an identity assumed by a workload from its runtime with short-lived tokens issued by the cloud and nothing stored. Acceptable is a short-lived credential obtained through a federated trust, where the secret is a cryptographic exchange rather than a stored value. Not acceptable is a static long-lived credential regardless of how it is stored, because the property that matters is whether a leaked artifact grants durable access, and storing it in a secrets manager is mitigation rather than satisfaction. Persistently is a defined term and does not mean continuously: activities may occur irregularly with intentional, documented interruptions provided status is always known, so an automated conformance check on the required cycle satisfies review on its own terms. What automation cannot review is the standard itself, which is reviewed on change and stated rather than claimed as automated. Scope sits between APM, which takes human identities, and ELP and JIT, which take privilege: SNU is the credential and the trust, not what they can reach. This is where the two-cloud architecture is resolved, since the GCP pipeline reaching AWS by stored key would fail the stated standard and is therefore built as workload identity federation. Fails on any static long-lived credential, any user-managed service account key, any trust policy broader than the intended workload, any cross-cloud access by stored credential, any credential unused within the window, or any identity present in the inventory and absent from the review.
- **1. Workload identity for compute** — Instance profiles and task roles assumed from the runtime. No access keys issued to workloads. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Service account attachment** — Service accounts attached to workloads directly. Key creation disabled by organization policy. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Cross-cloud federation** — GCP service account exchanges an identity token for short-lived AWS credentials via OIDC trust. No stored secret on either side. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Trust constraint** — Role trust policies scoped to a specific subject and audience. No wildcard principals. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Organization policy guardrail** — Service account key creation denied at organization level, so the failure mode is prevented rather than detected. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — No IAM users with access keys exist for workload purposes. Source: AWS iam list-users, list-access-keys. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.verify.1`: automated by `iam-snu-cfg-aws-no-user-access-keys`.*
- **CFG** — Every service account has zero user-managed keys. Source: GCP IAM service account keys, filtered to user-managed. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.verify.2`: automated by `iam-snu-cfg-gcp-no-user-managed-sa-keys`.*
- **CFG** — Org policy denying service account key creation is enforced, no exempted project. Source: GCP Organization Policy. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.verify.3`: not automated -- inapplicable. An organization policy needs an organization. GCP has no organization in this project (a standalone project under a personal billing account), so organization-level services do not exist here. Risk: Creating a service account key is not denied by policy. Declared state forbids it (policy rule GCP-IAM-02, on every push) and the live project is checked for user-managed keys daily (iam-snu-cfg-gcp-no-user-managed-sa-keys), so a key would be caught, not prevented.*
- **CFG** — Role trust policies name a specific subject and audience, no wildcard principals. Source: AWS iam get-role trust policy documents. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.verify.4`: automated by `iam-snu-cfg-aws-role-trusts-pinned`.*
- **CFG** — Federation pool and provider configured with the expected issuer and subject constraint. Source: AWS OIDC provider config; GCP workload identity pool. - **OPS** — Cross-cloud access observed using an assumed role via federation, not a static credential. Source: CloudTrail AssumeRoleWithWebIdentity events. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.validate.1`: automated by `svc-vcm-ops-aws-cross-cloud-federated`.*
- **OPS** — Zero authentication events using long-lived credentials, standing query, zero results expected. Source: CloudTrail sigv4 events attributable to a static access key. - **OPS** — Every non-user identity in the inventory appears in the credential review, zero unaccounted. Source: Reconciliation against the inventory generated for KSI-PIY-GIV. Not covered: Every non-user identity has a recorded credential position; there is no periodic credential review beyond the daily checks on keys, trusts and sessions.*
- **OPS** — Credentials unused within the window are surfaced by name. Source: AWS IAM credential report last-used; GCP service account last authentication. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-SNU.validate.4`: partly automated by `iam-snu-cfg-aws-roles-last-used`. Not covered: AWS roles; GCP service accounts' last authentication is not read. Session roles exist only during sessions, so they are judged only then.*

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by creating a disposable service account key in a non-production project and confirming the collector fails it, then deleting it and confirming the collector passes. Sufficiency is bounded by attribution: CloudTrail can identify the credential type behind an event, but a credential used entirely outside these two clouds would be invisible, so the claim is scoped to authentication against AWS and GCP rather than to all use of any credential that might exist.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Attribution is limited to authentication events against the two clouds in scope. An organization policy can be changed by someone with sufficient privilege, so the guardrail is a strong control rather than an absolute one, and that privilege question belongs to ELP. The standard defining appropriately secure is this project's construction, defended by the durability argument, and an assessor could reasonably hold a different line, particularly on whether a static key with short rotation in a secrets manager is acceptable. The synthetic environment has few non-user identities, so the coverage claim is easier to satisfy here than it would be at real scale.

## KSI-IAM-ELP — Partially Implemented

### Implementation

- Need is a fact about intent and configuration cannot express intent, so no policy document states whether a granted permission is necessary. The gap is closed by supplying the intent baseline explicitly: five roles declared as versioned code, each with what it requires and what it must not reach. Conformance against a declared standard is then checkable where an affirmative claim about need would not be. The roles are security engineer (read across both clouds and the compliance tooling, no production customer data), software engineer (deploy to the AWS application tier, no GCP analytics, no IAM mutation), data analyst (BigQuery query access, no AWS access at all), platform engineer (Terraform apply and the sole holder of IAM and organization policy mutation), and support (read-only application logs, no customer data). The prohibitions carry the evidential weight: that a software engineer can deploy proves little, whereas the data analyst having zero AWS presence is a specific falsifiable claim, so the collector is built primarily around prohibition checks expressed as zero-results queries. GCP primitive roles are barred outright, since binding owner, editor or viewer makes a least-privilege claim indefensible regardless of what else is configured. Persistently reviewed follows the reading set in SNU: automated conformance checking on the required cycle satisfies review, and the role model itself is reviewed on change. This indicator resolves the guardrail question SNU deferred, since platform engineer is the sole role holding organization policy mutation and any other principal holding it is a finding. Scope is the standing grant, what an identity can reach at rest; the credential belongs to APM and SNU, lifecycle to AAM, elevation timing to JIT.
- **1. Role definitions as code** — Five roles with explicit allow and deny sets, versioned in the repository as the intent baseline. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Permission sets** — IAM Identity Center permission sets, one per role. No inline or directly attached policies. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. IAM bindings** — Custom roles per declared role, bound at project scope. No primitive roles (owner, editor, viewer). *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. IAM mutation restriction** — Only platform engineer holds IAM and organization policy mutation, enforced by policy. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Access analysis** — IAM Access Analyzer enabled; GCP Policy Analyzer queries for effective access. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Every identity maps to exactly one declared role. Source: Role definitions versus IAM Identity Center assignments and GCP bindings. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-ELP.verify.1`: partly automated by `iam-elp-ops-identities-placed`. Not covered: Each identity maps to one register entry, and so to one lane; the entry's lane is not compared with the identity's live permissions.*
- **CFG** — No wildcard actions or resources in any attached policy. Source: AWS IAM policy documents; GCP custom role definitions. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-ELP.verify.2`: not automated -- gap. The CI read roles hold wildcard read actions by service (ec2:Describe*, s3:Get*) on Resource "*", enumerated per service rather than per call: Terraform's refresh calls more actions than can be listed stably. Write actions are scoped to named resources throughout; no policy grants a wildcard write or "*" action. Risk: A new read action AWS adds under a granted prefix is granted automatically; reads only.*
- **CFG** — No GCP primitive roles bound to any in-scope principal. Source: GCP IAM policy bindings. ### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by granting a disposable identity a permission its role prohibits and confirming the collector fails it, then removing it and confirming the collector passes. The prohibition queries are separately tested by generating a matching access event and confirming the zero-results query stops returning zero, which proves the query is watching rather than trivially empty; a zero-results check that would return zero regardless of reality is worthless and nothing in its output distinguishes the two cases. Sufficiency is bounded by effective-access computation: both clouds' analyzers resolve effective permissions, but resource-level conditions and nested group membership can produce access paths they approximate rather than resolve exactly.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** The role model is asserted. Conformance to it is enforced and provable; whether a software engineer genuinely needs a given permission is a claim this project makes rather than demonstrates. The gap narrowed from need being inexpressible to need being declared and enforced with the declaration asserted, which is the same position a real provider occupies. Device-scoped least privilege is not demonstrated: the persona has no managed device fleet, so the "or device" half of the statement is unaddressed rather than partially met. Effective access is computed by provider analyzers whose resolution of conditional and inherited paths is approximate. The synthetic environment has five roles and few resources, so conformance is easier to achieve than at real scale, where role sprawl is the actual failure mode.

### Tests

- Negative control for `iam-elp-cfg-aws-iam-mutation-principals` (collector/self_test.py): configurations that must fail it: a workload can, the read-only role can.

## KSI-IAM-JIT — Implemented

### Implementation

- Least-privileged and role-based are established by KSI-IAM-ELP and are referenced here rather than re-derived; this determination covers what JIT adds, which is attribute-based authorization and non-standing privilege. Just-in-time does not mean all privilege is requested on demand. A model where every action requires elevation trains people to elevate reflexively and makes the request meaningless, so the model is mixed and the lane split is the substance: security engineer holds standing read and elevates for incident response write; software engineer holds standing deploy; data analyst holds standing BigQuery query access and elevates for production datasets; support holds standing log read; platform engineer holds no standing privilege at all and elevates for IAM mutation, organization policy mutation and Terraform apply. That last line closes a loop across three indicators: SNU depends on an organization policy guardrail, ELP established platform engineer as its sole holder, and JIT removes it as a standing grant, so the guardrail can only be changed by someone who requested and was approved for the ability to change it, with a justification recorded. Attribute-based is constructed here as requiring at least a time condition and a resource-scope condition on every elevated grant, so an elevation is never simply this role now but this role against this scope until this moment. The two clouds diverge in capability and the determination states it rather than smoothing it: GCP provides a native entitlement model in Privileged Access Manager covering requesters, durations, justification, approval and automatic revocation, while AWS has no native equivalent and the documented alternatives are an AWS-published solution or an external identity provider's PIM. The latter is unavailable here for a specific reason: Google Cloud Identity is this project's identity provider and has no privileged identity management equivalent, unlike Entra, so there is nothing to integrate. The AWS side is therefore built as a state machine. For non-user accounts, a workload assuming a role for the duration of a task is just-in-time authorization by construction, satisfying the requirement without a separate elevation gate; this is an interpretation and is flagged as such. Persistently reviewed follows the reading set in SNU.
- **1. PAM entitlements** — One per elevated grant. Requesters, roles with IAM Conditions, maximum duration, justification required, approval required for organization-policy-adjacent roles. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Elevated permission sets** — Defined but assigned to no one by default. Session duration bounded. Condition keys evaluated against Identity Center session tags. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Elevation workflow** — Step Functions state machine: record justification, create account assignment, wait for TTL, delete assignment. SDK integrations rather than custom code where available. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Standing lane** — Permission sets and bindings for standing roles per the lane model. Platform engineer holds none. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Revocation backstop** — Scheduled reconciliation deleting any elevated assignment older than its TTL, independent of the workflow that created it. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*

Risk: GCP administration is standing, not just-in-time: the owner's Google account holds it permanently, behind Google's 2SV.*

- **Automated evidence.** 8 collector check(s) in the run on 2026-10-26: 8 PASS. Historical metrics (SDR-CSX-KMT) cover 19 day(s) of CI runs since 2026-10-02, in the metrics evidence object below; the year is what exists, not a full year.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by requesting a real elevation with a short TTL and confirming in sequence that the collector reports an active grant during the window, reports no grant after expiry, and fails if the assignment is still present after expiry. The zero-results queries are self-tested by deliberately leaving an assignment in place past TTL and confirming the query stops returning zero. Sufficiency is bounded by correlation fidelity: matching an audit event to a grant window depends on clock alignment and on the event carrying the assuming principal, so events where the principal cannot be resolved are reported as indeterminate rather than passed.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** The AWS elevation workflow is built rather than provided, so it carries operational risk a managed service would not: if the state machine fails between assignment and revocation, privilege persists until the backstop runs, which narrows the window rather than eliminating it. The non-user claim rests on an interpretation; treating a workload's short-lived assumed role as inherently just-in-time is defensible but is an argument rather than a mechanism, and an assessor could hold that non-user accounts require their own elevation gate. Approval is thin by construction: with a two-person security team an approver may be the requester's only colleague, so separation is nominal rather than real, which is a property of the persona rather than the design. The synthetic environment generates few elevation events, so correlation evidence is sparse compared to real operation.

## KSI-IAM-AAM — Implemented

### Implementation

- Using automation is unusually prescriptive for this catalog, which normally states an outcome and leaves the method open, so the interpretive room is narrow and the sharp question is what separates automated lifecycle management from a manual process with tooling in front of it. The position taken is that automation means a single authoritative event propagates without further human action. Declaring users in Terraform and applying to two clouds is automated provisioning but not automated lifecycle, because nothing happens unless someone remembers to open the change; suspending an account in Cloud Identity and having deprovisioning reach AWS over SCIM is lifecycle automation, because propagation is the system's job rather than a person's. Privileges are already built by ELP and JIT and are referenced rather than rebuilt; what AAM adds is that privilege changes flow from the same authoritative source as identity changes, so a role change is a reviewed code change rather than a console edit. Roles and groups is where the honest gap sits: SCIM sync from Google covers users only and group provisioning is not supported, so groups and permission set assignments are declared in Terraform. That is what the vendor documentation prescribes rather than a workaround, and it is stated as two mechanisms rather than presented as one unified pipeline. Securely is undefined and is read as a property of the mechanism rather than the outcome: the pipeline that manages accounts is itself an access path, so the SCIM bearer token is subject to SNU's credential durability standard and the Terraform identity that mutates Identity Center is subject to ELP's restriction and JIT's elevation requirement. Scope is the account existing, changing and ceasing to exist; the credential belongs to APM, non-user credentials to SNU, standing grants to ELP, elevation to JIT.
- **1. SCIM user provisioning** — Cloud Identity to Identity Center. Auto-provisioning enabled, deprovisioning on suspend and delete, attribute mapping complete. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform where API-supported)*
- **2. Group and assignment management** — Identity Center groups and permission set assignments declared in code, closing the documented SCIM group gap. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Cloud Identity users and groups** — Users and groups declared against the Cloud Identity directory. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Drift detection** — Reconciliation of live Identity Center state against declared state and against Cloud Identity. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. SCIM token rotation** — Access token rotated on schedule, expiry monitored, alert raised before expiry. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Identity source is the external provider and auto-provisioning is enabled. Source: AWS Identity Center settings. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-IAM-AAM.verify.1`: not automated -- descoped. SCIM provisioning (KSI-IAM-AAM) was descoped on 2026-10-03: it needs Cloud Identity Premium and mostly proves group sync for a two-person setup. The one Identity Center user is created by hand. Risk: Deprovisioning does not propagate: a user suspended in Google keeps its Identity Center user until removed by hand. Sign-in goes through Google's SAML, so a suspended account cannot start a session; one already open lasts up to its limit, 4 hours read-only or 1 hour elevated.*
- **CFG** — SCIM access token present and not within the expiry warning window. Source: Identity Center provisioning configuration. ### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested end to end: create a disposable Cloud Identity account, confirm it appears in Identity Center, suspend it, confirm it is deprovisioned, and confirm the collector reports each state correctly at each step. The zero-results queries are self-tested by making a direct console change and confirming the drift query stops returning zero. Sufficiency is bounded by propagation timing: SCIM is asynchronous, so a check running immediately after a lifecycle event can report a stale state truthfully, and the collector therefore reports within-window and beyond-window separately rather than treating any lag as a failure.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Group provisioning is not automated end to end. SCIM covers users only, so groups are declared in Terraform and the two mechanisms are stated rather than presented as one pipeline. SCIM propagation is asynchronous with no guaranteed bound, so there is always a window in which a suspended account retains AWS access; the window is measured and reported rather than assumed to be zero. Root and break-glass identities sit outside the lifecycle pipeline by design, since they exist for the case where the pipeline or its identity provider is unavailable, and are enumerated and monitored rather than managed. The synthetic environment has no real joiners or leavers, so lifecycle events are exercised deliberately rather than observed occurring.

## KSI-IAM-SUS — Implemented

### Implementation

- Privileged account is a defined term and its note extends it to privileged roles and other capabilities used to assign privilege, so the population is not a list of people. It resolves into five categories: top-level administrative (AWS root, GCP organization admin) and break-glass, both declared by name; privileged roles, declared in Terraform per the ELP model; active JIT elevations, which are live state and change continuously; and privileged automation, meaning the responder, the Terraform apply identity and the SCIM provisioning principal, each of which holds administrative capability affecting availability. The last two categories mean the enumeration is resolved at run time rather than trusted from a file. Suspicious is constructed from two sources so the definition is neither purely ours nor purely bought: managed threat detection supplies behavioural findings, and a small environment-specific rule set covers what the vendor will not flag but this environment cares about, being root console login, IAM change outside the Terraform pipeline, elevation on a long-unused role, and repeated failed authentication against a privileged identity. Response is graduated, which the statement's disjunction licenses: notify at medium, contain at high by attaching an explicit deny and revoking sessions, and suspend the Cloud Identity account at critical, which propagates to AWS through the SCIM pipeline built for AAM so one action reaches both clouds. Response is automatic with fast undo rather than approval-gated, because a two-person team has no credible overnight approval coverage and containment is reversible by construction. Responder privilege is split rather than granted wholesale: standing capability for reversible containment only, with anything destructive requiring human action through JIT. Response is itself a defined term meaning tracking, evaluating, mitigating, monitoring, remediating, assessing exploitation and reporting, so a responder that disables an account and stops satisfies the English and not the definition; every action writes a record of finding, evaluation, action, timestamp and outcome. Automation is not required by this statement, unlike AAM where the mechanism was named; it is a design choice made because Class C requires the indicator be verified on the 3-day cycle and manual response for this team is not credibly prompt. The responder is itself a privileged account and cannot secure itself: its actions are written to storage it cannot modify, and changes to it require JIT elevation, leaving the residual that a compromised responder can still contain identities it should not.
- **1. Managed threat detection** — GuardDuty foundational only. Optional protection plans disabled to keep cost near zero at sandbox volume. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Managed threat detection** — Security Command Center, free tier. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Environment-specific rules** — Root console login; IAM change outside the pipeline; elevation on a long-unused role; repeated failed authentication against a privileged identity. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Severity routing** — Medium notifies. High contains. Critical suspends at the identity provider. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Responder** — Deny policy attach and session revoke; Cloud Identity suspend at critical. Root and break-glass excluded by name. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **6. Responder identity** — Reversible containment capability only. No delete, no grant, no create. Declared standing exception to ELP and JIT. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **7. Response record** — Finding, evaluation, action, timestamp and outcome, written to storage the responder identity cannot modify. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **8. Privileged enumeration** — Run-time resolution across all five categories, reconciled against effective-permission analysis. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested per severity tier using sample findings, confirming the full chain fires at each: finding produced, routed, correct tier action taken, access stopped where applicable, record written to immutable storage. Then negatively, by generating a finding against an excluded identity and confirming no action occurs. The undo path is tested by reversing a containment and confirming access is restored, since a reversible design that has never been reversed is an assumption rather than a property. Zero-results queries are self-tested. Sufficiency is bounded by detection coverage: the responder acts on what the vendor model and the local rules surface, so the claim is scoped to detected activity rather than to all suspicious activity.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Detection coverage bounds the claim, which is response to detected suspicious activity rather than to all of it. The responder is a standing exception to this project's own privilege model, narrowed to reversible containment but real; a compromised responder can contain identities it should not, and immutable logging makes that visible rather than prevented. Root and break-glass are excluded from automated response, so the two most privileged accounts carry human response times. Severity mapping is this project's construction and a different provider would map tiers differently. The synthetic environment produces no genuine suspicious activity, so the path is exercised with sample findings rather than observed operating.

## KSI-PIY-GIV — Partially Implemented

### Implementation

- Information Resource takes its meaning from 44 USC 3502(6) and expressly covers non-machine-based resources such as organizational policies, procedures and employees alongside machine-based ones; the term's note makes a requirement that does not specify a type inclusive of both. GIV carries no type qualifier, so its literal scope includes the non-machine half, which is out of scope project-wide. The indicator is therefore partially satisfied and the gap is declared rather than removed by redefining the term. Authoritative and real-time are undefined and are interpreted here: authoritative means the system of record for a resource's existence, so the provider's own asset service rather than Terraform state, because state describes intent and drifts from reality while the asset service reports what exists; real-time means generated at query time from live sources rather than served from cache, which is what "when needed" implies. Within the machine-based half, the meaning of "all" is inherited from MAS-CSO-IIR: resources within the identified cloud service offering, not everything in the accounts. Fails if any in-scope machine-based resource is absent from the generated inventory, if the inventory comes from a non-authoritative source, if generation requires manual assembly or merging, or if it cannot be produced on demand. One unaccounted resource fails the indicator, with no percentage threshold, because completeness is the entire value this indicator provides to the rest of the catalog.
- **1. AWS Config recorder** — All supported resource types, all in-scope regions, continuous recording. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Cloud Asset Inventory** — Asset feed at organization scope, all supported asset types. *(GCP; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Inventory generator** — Single command producing a normalized inventory across both clouds at query time. No cached intermediate. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Config recorder enabled, recording all supported types, in every in-scope region. Source: AWS Config describe-configuration-recorders and recorder status. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-GIV.verify.1`: partly automated by `piy-giv-cfg-aws-config-recorder`. Not covered: By design, the recorder records a declared list of resource types, not all supported types: Config bills per item recorded and the environment is rebuilt often (DECISIONS.md, 2026-09-05). That the live recorder matches the declared list is shown by svc-acm-ops-aws-no-drift, not here. Only us-east-1 is in scope.*
- **CFG** — Asset feed enabled at organization scope. Source: GCP Cloud Asset Inventory. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-GIV.verify.2`: partly automated by `piy-giv-cfg-gcp-asset-feed`. Not covered: The feed is at project scope. The project sits outside any organization, so organization scope does not exist here; the row's wording assumes one.*
- **OPS** — Generated inventory reconciled against live independent enumeration per cloud, zero resources present in one and absent from the other. Source: AWS Config and Cloud Asset Inventory versus direct service-level list calls. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-GIV.validate.1`: partly automated by `piy-giv-ops-aws-inventory-current`, `piy-giv-ops-aws-inventory-min-buckets`, `piy-giv-ops-gcp-inventory-current`. Not covered: One direction only: everything the AWS inventory lists exists in its service. The other direction, live resources absent from the inventory, is not enumerated. A floor on the count of S3 buckets in the inventory, not a reconciliation against a direct listing. One direction only: everything the GCP inventory lists exists in its service. The other direction, live resources absent from the inventory, is not enumerated.*
- **OPS** — Inventory generated at query time returns a resource created after the previous generation. Source: Timestamp comparison against a freshly created resource. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-GIV.validate.2`: not automated -- gap. Not run deliberately: creating a resource outside Terraform to watch the inventory pick it up would leave it in AWS Config's record for the 27 to 51 hours Config takes to record a deletion, failing the declared-inventory checks meanwhile (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-02). The inventory is generated at query time from Config every run. Risk: That a new resource appears in the next generation is shown in practice by every session, not by a recorded test.*
- **OPS** — Every in-scope region and project appears in the output, zero in-scope locations missing. Source: Region and project enumeration versus inventory coverage. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-GIV.validate.3`: partly automated by `piy-giv-ops-gcp-inventory-has-project`. Not covered: Confirms the one GCP project appears. AWS regions are not enumerated against the inventory.*
- **Automated evidence.** 6 collector check(s) in the run on 2026-10-26: 6 PASS. Historical metrics (SDR-CSX-KMT) cover 19 day(s) of CI runs since 2026-10-02, in the metrics evidence object below; the year is what exists, not a full year.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by seeding a resource that should appear and one that should be excluded, then confirming the generator returns exactly one. Sufficiency is bounded to what the authoritative sources support: the generator can only report resource types AWS Config and Cloud Asset Inventory track, so supported-type coverage is the stated ceiling and any unsupported type is a declared gap rather than a silent absence.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Non-machine-based information resources are out of scope project-wide, so this indicator is partially satisfied. The residual risk is that a real provider could not demonstrate complete inventory coverage on this basis, since the non-machine half of the definition would remain unaccounted. Coverage of the machine-based half is bounded by what the authoritative sources support; anything outside their supported types is invisible to this measure.

## KSI-PIY-RVD — Partially Implemented

### Implementation

- The statement presupposes the program exists, and the rules make it close to mandatory rather than optional: the vulnerability detection rule requires providers to discover vulnerabilities using appropriate techniques such as assessment, scanning, threat intelligence, vulnerability disclosure mechanisms, bug bounties and penetration testing, naming disclosure mechanisms inside a MUST, and the reporting rules expect high-level overviews of disclosure and bug bounty activity. The program itself is concrete and cheap: a published policy stating scope, boundaries, safe harbour and expected response times; a security.txt served at the well-known path with a contact, a policy link and a maintained expiry; an intake path reaching a monitored destination; and a triage flow. That last element is where the substance is, because a disclosure program terminating in an inbox is a facade: the program's value is that an external report enters the same response pipeline as a scanner finding, with the same severity model and the same remediation timeframes. Effectiveness needs distinguishing from existence, as it did in CMT-RVP and CNA-RVP. Publishing the file is existence; effectiveness is measured by acknowledgement and triage times, the proportion of reports duplicating findings already known from scanning, and the proportion that were valid and undetectable by any other mechanism, which is the program's real value. The most important and most often broken signal is whether the published contact actually works, since a bouncing address or an expired file means the program does not exist regardless of the policy text, and both are directly testable. The honest problem is the same shape as CNA-RVP's: this environment receives no real reports, so effectiveness signals are computed over an empty set. What can be demonstrated is that the program exists, that the intake works end to end when exercised, and that a submitted report enters the response pipeline correctly.
- **1. Published disclosure policy** — Scope, in and out of bounds, safe harbour, expected response times. Versioned. *(Repo; provisioned by Version control)*
- **2. security.txt** — Served at the well-known path with contact, policy link, preferred languages and a maintained expiry. Published by the load balancer as a fixed response rather than by the application, so the disclosure channel survives an application outage and no unauthenticated public path enters request handling. Expiry carried by a rotating timestamp on a ninety-day period, not the apply timestamp -- a value that re-evaluated every plan would leave KSI-SVC-ACM's drift signal permanently positive. Policy line omitted entirely when no URL is published. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Monitored intake** — Contact reaching a destination that alerts, not an unwatched mailbox. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Triage flow** — Received reports entering the same response pipeline as scanner findings, with the same severity model and remediation timeframes. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Program metrics** — Acknowledgement time, triage time, duplicate rate against known findings, and unique-finding rate. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **6. Contact liveness check** — Automated test that a submission to the published contact arrives. Cheap, and it catches the failure that silently kills these programs. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **7. Effectiveness signal report** — Section emitted by the shared signal report generator, aggregating this indicator's program metrics over the period. One generator serves all indicators that declare signals; no per-indicator scheduled job. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days, 3 months. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Disclosure policy is published and versioned. Source: Repository and published location. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.verify.1`: not automated -- gap. No disclosure policy is published. security.txt names a contact; its Policy field is optional and unset (infra/aws/edge.tf, security_policy_url). Risk: A researcher has a contact but no stated scope, safe harbour or response expectation.*
- **CFG** — security.txt is present at the well-known path and well-formed. Source: Fetch and parse the published file. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.verify.2`: partly automated by `piy-rvd-cfg-aws-security-txt` (judged only while aws-phase1 stands). Not covered: Served only while the environment stands; between sessions there is no published file, so the program's contact is unreachable then too.*
- **CFG** — Published contact resolves to a monitored destination. Source: Intake configuration. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.verify.3`: not automated -- gap. The published contact is on caliper.elvievalmores.com, which has no mail exchanger, so mail to it bounces (PROJECT-CONTEXT open items). Risk: A report sent to the published contact is lost.*
- **CFG** — Triage flow is configured into the vulnerability response pipeline. Source: Workflow configuration. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.verify.4`: not automated -- gap. There is no intake to route from, so no triage flow is configured into the vulnerability response pipeline. Risk: A received report would be handled ad hoc, if it arrived at all.*
- **OPS** — A test submission to the published contact arrives at the monitored destination. Source: Automated liveness test. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.validate.1`: not automated -- gap. With no mail exchanger there is no destination for a test submission to reach. Risk: As above: the contact is not live, so the program's intake does not function.*
- **OPS** — security.txt expiry is in the future, standing query, zero expired results expected. Source: Fetch and parse the published file. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.validate.2`: partly automated by `piy-rvd-cfg-aws-security-txt` (judged only while aws-phase1 stands). Not covered: As verify 2: the expiry is checked when the file is served, in sessions.*
- **OPS** — A test report entering the intake reaches the response pipeline with a severity assigned. Source: End-to-end test. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.validate.3`: not automated -- gap. No intake and no triage flow exist, so there is no end-to-end path to test. Risk: The report-to-response path is unproven.*
- **OPS** — Every received report has an acknowledgement and a triage outcome recorded. Source: Report records. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.validate.4`: not automated -- gap. No reports are received or recorded; the signal report lists reports received as not collected, unknown rather than zero. Risk: A report and its handling would leave no record to check.*
- **OPS** — Signal report section generated for the period, with signals computed including nil results. Source: Signal report output. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RVD.validate.5`: automated by `piy-rvd-ops-signal-report`.*
- **Automated evidence.** 2 collector check(s) in the run on 2026-10-26: 1 NOT_STANDING, 1 PASS. Historical metrics (SDR-CSX-KMT) cover 19 day(s) of CI runs since 2026-10-02, in the metrics evidence object below; the year is what exists, not a full year.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by submitting a test report and confirming it arrives, is acknowledged, is triaged and enters the response pipeline with a severity assigned. The expiry check is tested separately by letting the expiry lapse in a test copy and confirming it fails. Sufficiency is bounded by report volume, which is zero: the mechanism is testable end to end, whereas its effectiveness at surfacing vulnerabilities other mechanisms miss is not, since that requires researchers who have no reason to look at this environment.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Effectiveness cannot be demonstrated, only existence and function, since no real reports arrive and the signals that would show whether the program adds coverage are computed over an empty set; this is the same shape as CNA-RVP, where protection could be exercised but not proven operationally effective. There is no bug bounty: the rules name bounties alongside disclosure programs, and a paid programme is out of scope for a persona with no budget, which connects to the reasoning that defers the investment indicator in this cluster. Safe harbour language is published but untested, since no researcher has relied on it. The synthetic environment has no external attack surface worth researching, so even a real program attached to it would receive nothing. The periodic human review step is out of scope project-wide: judging whether signals indicate a problem is organizational activity, the same category as the personnel and training material excluded at KSI-PIY-GIV. Signal generation is machine-based and is built; the judgment on top is declared, not performed.

### Tests

- Negative control for `piy-rvd-ops-signal-report` (collector/self_test.py): inputs that must fail it: no report, a section missing, a signal not computed, a signal absent, stale.
- Designed, not yet automated: A test submission to the published contact arrives at the monitored destination (Automated liveness test).
- Designed, not yet automated: A test report entering the intake reaches the response pipeline with a severity assigned (End-to-end test).

## KSI-PIY-RSD — Partially Implemented

### Implementation

- Secure By Design is three principles: take ownership of customer security outcomes, embrace radical transparency and accountability, and lead from the top. The catalog says principles rather than pledge, and the distinction matters: the principles are the framework and constitute a posture claim, whereas the pledge is a separate voluntary commitment of seven goals that the publishing agency neither enforces nor verifies. The subject shift from earlier indicators is what makes this tractable. Input validation, error handling and memory protection were declared under CNA-MAT as the application layer an infrastructure collector cannot inspect, and mobile code and information in shared resources were handled similarly under CNA-DFP and SVC-PRR; those indicators asked whether the running system has the property, whereas this one asks whether the development process builds it in, and a process leaves artifacts. Alignment is therefore claimed through what already exists rather than through a policy document. Ownership of customer security outcomes is evidenced by secure defaults in the declared baselines, since encryption, absence of public access, enumerated policies and digest pinning are not opt-in. Transparency and accountability is evidenced by the published disclosure policy and by the decision log as a contemporaneous record of security reasoning. Lead from the top is not evidenced, being organizational and belonging to the executive support indicator in this cluster, so alignment is claimed on two of three principles. Security in the lifecycle is evidenced by the pipeline rather than a written lifecycle document: policy evaluation before merge, signature and provenance verification before deploy, dependency and image scanning in CI, stage chaining where failure stops progression, and signed commits with branch protection. What this indicator adds is the claim that these constitute a lifecycle rather than a collection, plus the review of whether they work. The design-time mappings covering security engineering principles and security architecture leave artifacts only if someone produces them, and the decision log is the closest this project has; that claim is defensible and also self-serving, so it is flagged rather than presented plainly.
- **1. Lifecycle control map** — Each lifecycle stage mapped to the security control operating at it, sourced from existing indicators rather than duplicated. *(Repo; provisioned by Version control)*
- **2. Principle position register** — Each of the three principles with its evidence, or a recorded reason it is not evidenced. *(Repo; provisioned by Version control)*
- **3. Secure defaults enforcement** — Policy rules asserting that declared baselines are secure without customer action. *(Repo; provisioned by Version control)*
- **4. Static analysis** — Running in CI with findings routed to the vulnerability pipeline. *(Repo; provisioned by CI)*
- **5. Design reasoning capture** — Posture-changing changes require a recorded rationale, checked at merge. This is what makes the decision log a required artifact rather than a habit. *(Repo; provisioned by CI)*
- **6. Effectiveness signal report** — Section emitted by the shared signal report generator, aggregating this indicator's signals over the period. One generator serves all indicators that declare signals; no per-indicator scheduled job. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 months. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Every lifecycle stage has a mapped security control. Source: Stage map versus pipeline definition. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.verify.1`: automated by `piy-rsd-cfg-lifecycle-controls`.*
- **CFG** — Each principle carries a position with evidence or a reason. Source: Repository file, checked in CI. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.verify.2`: automated by `piy-rsd-cfg-principles-positioned`.*
- **CFG** — Static analysis is configured in the pipeline. Source: Workflow definition. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.verify.3`: partly automated by `mla-evc-cfg-evaluates-plan-json`. Not covered: Static analysis of the infrastructure only -- Trivy and the authored rules over every push's plans; the application code has no static analyser.*
- **CFG** — Design reasoning check is configured at merge. Source: Branch protection and workflow configuration. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.verify.4`: not automated -- inapplicable. The project pushes to main rather than merging pull requests, so there is no merge to gate. Design reasoning is recorded in docs/DECISIONS.md as practice, not enforced at any gate. Risk: A posture-changing change can land without its reasoning recorded; nothing checks that every one has an entry.*
- **OPS** — Zero lifecycle stages without a security control, standing query, zero results expected. Source: Stage map versus pipeline definition. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.validate.1`: automated by `piy-rsd-cfg-lifecycle-controls`.*
- **OPS** — Zero posture-changing changes without recorded reasoning, standing query, zero results expected. Source: Change records versus decision entries. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.validate.2`: not automated -- gap. Nothing correlates posture-changing commits with DECISIONS.md entries. The log is kept by practice, and it is extensive, but its completeness is not checked. Risk: An undocumented posture change would not be flagged.*
- **OPS** — Static analysis has run on every merge. Source: Pipeline execution history. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.validate.3`: partly automated by `mla-evc-ops-every-push-evaluated`. Not covered: Static analysis of the infrastructure only -- Trivy and the authored rules over every push's plans; the application code has no static analyser. There are no merges; every push to main is what is evaluated.*
- **OPS** — Secure defaults hold in declared baselines. Source: Policy evaluation results from KSI-MLA-EVC. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.validate.4`: automated by `mla-evc-ops-no-failed-push`.*
- **OPS** — Signal report section generated for the period, with signals computed including nil results. Source: Signal report output. Required every 3 months; implemented: Per review. *Row `KSI-PIY-RSD.validate.5`: automated by `piy-rsd-ops-signal-report`.*
- **Automated evidence.** 3 collector check(s) in the run on 2026-10-26: 3 PASS. Historical metrics (SDR-CSX-KMT) cover 19 day(s) of CI runs since 2026-10-02, in the metrics evidence object below; the year is what exists, not a full year.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by submitting a posture-changing change with no recorded reasoning and confirming the merge check fails it. Sufficiency is bounded at the code level: the pipeline can prove static analysis ran and that findings were handled, and it cannot prove that input validation is correct, that error handling does not leak, or that memory is protected, so those mappings remain unreachable as CNA-MAT already declared.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Lead from the top is not evidenced, since executive accountability is organizational and belongs to the executive support indicator, so alignment is claimed on two of three principles. Code-level mappings remain unreachable, and static analysis is evidence that checking happens rather than that the properties hold. Claiming this project's own decision log as design-time evidence is self-serving and is flagged as such: it is genuinely contemporaneous and genuinely records security reasoning, which is more than most projects have, and it is also an artifact this project happens to produce. No threat model exists as a distinct artifact, since design reasoning is recorded per decision rather than as a structured analysis. The statement names security and privacy, and the privacy half is unexercised because the environment holds synthetic data with no privacy interest. The periodic human review step is out of scope project-wide: judging whether signals indicate a problem is organizational activity, the same category as the personnel and training material excluded at KSI-PIY-GIV. Signal generation is machine-based and is built; the judgment on top is declared, not performed.

### Tests

- Negative control for `piy-rsd-cfg-lifecycle-controls` (collector/self_test.py): inputs that must fail it: a stage not built, no criterion, evidence that does not exist.
- Negative control for `piy-rsd-cfg-principles-positioned` (collector/self_test.py): inputs that must fail it: no reason, evidence that does not exist.
- Negative control for `piy-rsd-ops-signal-report` (collector/self_test.py): inputs that must fail it: no report, a section missing, a signal not computed, a signal absent, stale.

## KSI-PIY-RIS — Not Implemented

### Implementation

- **Deferred.** This was expected to defer cleanly on budget grounds and the mappings are more mixed than that, so the counter-argument is recorded rather than passed over. Information security resources and allocation of resources are funding activities that a persona with no budget cannot demonstrate, and separation of duties barely exists across two people. But control assessments, contingency plan and testing coordination, coordinated incident response testing, and supply chain plan updates are activities this project actually performs: it assesses controls continuously, tests recovery monthly and updates supply chain positions quarterly. That supports a reading where investment means effort and attention rather than money, with effectiveness measurable from automation coverage, gate efficacy and remediation time. That reading is rejected for two reasons. The statement says the provider's investments, and the two mappings that define what investment means in this control family are both explicitly about resources and funding, so reading it as effort would substitute a meaning the mappings do not support in order to have something to build. And the measurable signals under that reading are already measured under CMT-RVP and SVC-EIS, so building a third view of them under a different name would manufacture coverage rather than add it. Resulting risk to customers: a provider that does not review security investment has no mechanism ensuring security work is funded, prioritised or sustained when it competes with delivery, and every technical control in this project assumes someone keeps paying for it.
- **Resulting risk.** This is a deferral made against a live counter-argument rather than by default, and the argument is preserved because a different reader could reasonably reach the opposite conclusion. The deferral rests on the mappings defining investment as resource allocation, not on budgets being non-machine.

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 months. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **N/A** — No evidence produced. Source: Deferred. Required every 3 months; implemented: Not implemented.
- **Automated evidence.** No collector check exists for this indicator yet, so no evidence is attached. The rows above are the determination's design.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** This is a deferral made against a live counter-argument rather than by default, and the argument is preserved because a different reader could reasonably reach the opposite conclusion. The deferral rests on the mappings defining investment as resource allocation, not on budgets being non-machine.

### Tests

- None run or designed.

### Evidence

None attached.


## KSI-PIY-RES — Not Implemented

### Implementation

- **Deferred.** No control mappings at all, and the subject is an organizational relationship between people who do not exist. KSI-PIY-GIV also had no mappings and was still buildable because its subject was inventories; here the subject is executive sponsorship, which has no infrastructure expression. Unlike SVC-PRR, where the resource state half was machine-based even though plans and procedures were not, there is no machine-based sliver to keep: nothing about executive support leaves an artifact this project could check. One consequence reaches beyond this indicator. KSI-PIY-RSD claimed alignment with two of the three Secure By Design principles and recorded lead from the top as belonging here; this deferral means that principle is unevidenced across the project rather than merely unaddressed in one determination. Resulting risk to customers: a provider without demonstrated executive support has no mechanism ensuring security priorities survive contact with competing delivery pressure, which is the first thing to fail in a real organization and is invisible in a synthetic one.
- **Resulting risk.** This is the cleanest deferral in the project: no mappings, no machine-based subject, and no sliver. Its consequence is carried in KSI-PIY-RSD, where alignment with Secure By Design is claimed on two principles of three because the third depends on this one.

- **Limitations and residual risk.** This is the cleanest deferral in the project: no mappings, no machine-based subject, and no sliver. Its consequence is carried in KSI-PIY-RSD, where alignment with Secure By Design is claimed on two principles of three because the third depends on this one.

### Tests

- None run or designed.

### Evidence

None attached.


## KSI-SVC-ACM — Implemented

### Implementation

- This indicator scopes itself. Unlike KSI-PIY-GIV, which said all information resources and forced a partial determination, ACM says machine-based explicitly, so the project's exclusion of non-machine resources does not bite and the indicator can be fully satisfied. Drift is a defined term and is broader than configuration files: it covers deviation from the intended and assessed state, naming configurations, deployed software, privileges, running processes and availability as common forms. Declared-versus-live configuration is the primary mechanism, privilege drift is already detected by the ELP conformance checks, and deployed software is caught at resource-level configuration; running processes and availability are outside what configuration-level checking can see and are declared unaddressed rather than claimed. Managed using automation is the same prescriptive phrasing AAM used, and the reading established there applies: the declared state is authoritative and changes flow from it, so a resource whose configuration is not declared cannot be said to be managed. The word assessed in the drift definition carries a consequence: the baseline is not simply whatever was last applied but the state that was assessed, so drift detection and evidence collection must run against the same state rather than diverging from each other. Relationship to GIV: that indicator enumerates what exists, this one asserts everything enumerated is declared, so the coverage check is the inventory reconciled against declared state in both directions and a resource present in one and absent from the other is the headline failure. Persistently reviewed follows the reading set in SNU.
- **1. Declared infrastructure** — All machine-based resources declared in code. No console-created resources. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **2. Remote state backend** — Versioned, encrypted, with state locking. State is the declared baseline and is protected as a security-relevant asset. *(AWS; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **3. Drift detection** — Scheduled plan in check mode across both clouds. Exit status treated as the drift signal. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **4. Coverage reconciliation** — Inventory compared against declared state in both directions. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*
- **5. Change-path enforcement** — Configuration change events attributed to the pipeline principal; anything else surfaced. *(Both; provisioned by Terraform)*

### Validation

- **Cycle.** Required: 3 days. Current practice: the collector runs daily from GitHub Actions (.github/workflows/collect.yml), as a read-only identity in each cloud with no stored credential, and this record was emitted by that run. Daily is stricter than every required cycle above (DECISIONS.md, 2026-10-01).
- **CFG** — Remote state backend has versioning, encryption and locking enabled. Source: Backend bucket and lock configuration. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.verify.1`: automated by `svc-acm-cfg-aws-state-bucket`, `svc-acm-cfg-collect-init-locks-state`, `svc-acm-cfg-drift-init-locks-state`.*
- **CFG** — Drift detection schedule exists and is enabled. Source: Scheduler configuration. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.verify.2`: automated by `svc-acm-cfg-drift-scheduled`, `svc-acm-cfg-drift-workflow-active`.*
- **CFG** — Every resource in declared state exists in the environment. Source: Terraform state versus provider APIs. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.verify.3`: automated by `svc-acm-cfg-aws-declared-exists-live`, `svc-acm-cfg-gcp-declared-exists-live`.*
- **CFG** — Every machine-based resource in the inventory appears in declared state. Source: Inventory from KSI-PIY-GIV versus Terraform state. - **OPS** — Zero drift between declared and live configuration, standing query, zero results expected. Source: Terraform plan in check mode, exit status and diff. Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.validate.1`: automated by `svc-acm-ops-aws-no-drift`, `svc-acm-ops-gcp-no-drift`.*

GCP's Admin Activity logs are not in the corpus.*

Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.validate.3`: partly automated by `svc-acm-ops-out-of-band-change-detected`. Not covered: Run every 30 days by the operator and recorded, not every 3: the test changes or provokes the account, so it does not run unattended.*

Required every 3 days; implemented: Per run. *Row `KSI-SVC-ACM.validate.4`: partly automated by `iam-elp-ops-aws-iam-mutations-by-platform-engineer`. Not covered: IAM mutations outside the platform engineer surface through ELP's standing query; unused permissions (the other half of privilege drift) are not surfaced.*
- **Automated evidence.** 13 collector check(s) in the run on 2026-10-26: 13 PASS. Historical metrics (SDR-CSX-KMT) cover 19 day(s) of CI runs since 2026-10-02, in the metrics evidence object below; the year is what exists, not a full year.

### Assessment

- **No independent assessor is engaged.** The statements below are the provider's own assurance reasoning from the determination. They are not an independent assessment and should not be read as one.
- **Automation assurance.** Accuracy is tested by making a console change to a declared resource and confirming detection reports it, then reverting and confirming detection returns clean; a drift detector that has never detected drift is an assumption rather than a property. The mutation query is self-tested by making a change as a non-pipeline principal and confirming it stops returning zero. Sufficiency is bounded by what the declaring mechanism can express: Terraform describes resource configuration, not running process state or in-instance software inventory, so two of the five named forms of drift are outside this measure's reach.
- **Limitations and residual risk.** Two of the five named forms of drift, running processes and availability, are outside configuration-level detection and are declared unaddressed rather than folded into the claim. Drift detection runs on a cycle, so a change made and reverted between runs is invisible; the mutation query partly compensates by watching events rather than state, but only where the provider logs the change. The declared baseline is only as trustworthy as the state backend: a compromise of state would make drift detection report clean against a corrupted baseline.
